Conditional exponential directed last passage percolation under a one-point upper large deviation event
Jinho Baik, Dylan Cordaro, Tejaswi Tripathi

Abstract
Under typical scaling, the last passage time field of the directed last passage percolation model with exponential site distributions converges to the KPZ fixed point. In this paper, we consider an atypical scenario in which the last passage time to a specific site is unusually large, and we explore how the last passage time field changes under this one-point upper large deviation event. We prove a conditional law of large numbers and compute the limiting fluctuations in certain regimes. Our proofs rely on an analysis of explicit multi-point distributions.



1
Introduction and main results

1.1
Introduction

Let
{\mathbb{N}}
denote the set of natural numbers and set
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
.
For two points
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},p_{2})
and
\mathbf{q}=(q_{1},q_{2})
in
{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
satisfying
p_{1}\leq q_{1}
and
p_{2}\leq q_{2}
,
an up/right path from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
is a sequence

\pi=\{\mathbf{v}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}
with
r=q_{1}+q_{2}-p_{1}-p_{2}+1
, where

\mathbf{v}_{1}=\mathbf{p}
,
\mathbf{v}_{r}=\mathbf{q}
, and
\mathbf{v}_{i+1}-\mathbf{v}_{i}\in\{(1,0),(0,1)\}
for every
i
.

Definition 1.1

(Exponential LPP)
.

Let
\{\omega_{\mathbf{v}}:\mathbf{v}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\}
be a collection of i.i.d. exponential random variables of mean
1
.
The last passage time from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
is

\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{p}}(\mathbf{q})=\max_{\pi:\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}}E(\pi),\qquad E(\pi)=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\pi}\omega_{\mathbf{v}},

where the maximum is taken over all up/right paths from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
.
When
\mathbf{p}=(1,1)
, we write
\mathcal{L}_{(1,1)}(\mathbf{q})=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{q})
.
We call the 2-dimensional random field
\mathcal{L}=\{\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{q}):\mathbf{q}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}\}
the exponential last passage percolation, or simply exponential LPP.
For
(\alpha,\beta)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}
, we define

\mathcal{L}(\alpha,\beta)=\mathcal{L}(\lceil\alpha\rceil,\lceil\beta\rceil)

(1.1)

where
\lceil\alpha\rceil
denotes the smallest integer that is larger or equal to
\alpha
.

The exponential LPP is equivalent to several fundamental models in probability and statistical physics, including the corner growth model with wedge initial condition, the continuous-time totally asymmetric simple exclusion process (TASEP) with step initial condition, and the tandem queues model.
It is also an archetypal example of the KPZ universality class. Many results have been established for exponential LPP:

•

The law of large numbers was obtained by
[
18
]
: For every
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{L}(xN,yN)}{N}=\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x,y):=(\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y})^{2}

(1.2)

almost surely and also in probability.
We also set
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}(x,y)=(\sqrt{x-x^{\prime}}+\sqrt{y-y^{\prime}})^{2}
so that
\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x,y)=\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{(0,0)}(x,y)
.

•

Johansson proved the convergence of the one-point distributions in
[
8
]
:

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{L}(xN,yN)-\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x,y)N}{(xy)^{-1/6}(\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y})^{4/3}N^{1/3}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\TW_{2}

(1.3)

for every
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}
, where
\TW_{2}
is the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution.
Here,
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}
denotes convergence in distribution.

•

The two-dimensional random field also converges
[
15
]
. In particular, for given
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{+}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{L}\left(txN+s\frac{2x^{2/3}y^{1/6}}{(\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y})^{1/3}}N^{2/3},{t}yN-s\frac{2x^{1/6}y^{2/3}}{(\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y})^{1/3}}N^{2/3}\right)-\bar{\mathcal{L}}(tx,ty)N}{(xy)^{-1/6}(\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y})^{4/3}N^{1/3}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f.d.d.}}{{=\!=\!=}}\mathsf{H}_{\textnormal{step}}(s,t)

(1.4)

in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions for
(s,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
where
\mathsf{H}_{\textnormal{step}}
denotes the KPZ fixed point with narrow wedge initial condition.

•

The upper large deviation result was obtained by
[
8
]
:

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)>\ell N)=-\mathsf{J}(\ell)\qquad\text{for $\ell>\bar{\mathcal{L}}(a,b)$}

(1.5)

where
1
1

1

The paper
[
8
]
gives a variational formula for the rate function
\mathsf{J}(\ell)
. The variational formula can be solved to give the explicit formula. For example, see
[
10
, (46)]
for the case when
a=b
.

\mathsf{J}(\ell)=\sqrt{D}+a\log\bigg(\frac{\ell+a-b-\sqrt{D}}{\ell+a-b+\sqrt{D}}\bigg)+b\log\bigg(\frac{\ell-a+b-\sqrt{D}}{\ell-a+b+\sqrt{D}}\bigg)

(1.6)

with

D=\ell^{2}-2(a+b)\ell+(a-b)^{2}.

(1.7)

The same paper also obtained the corresponding lower large deviation result.
A hydrodynamic upper large deviation result was established in
[
17
]
(in the TASEP setting), extending works of
[
7
,
19
]
.
For a comprehensive list of works on large deviations in KPZ universality class models, see
[
4
]
.

The goal of this paper is to investigate the behavior of exponential LPP conditioned on the event that the last passage time at a specific site is larger than expected.
Let
a,b>0
, and suppose that
\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\ell N
for some
\ell>\bar{\mathcal{L}}(a,b)
.
Given this conditioning, what does the field
\{\mathcal{L}(xaN,ybN)\}_{(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}}
look like when
N
is large?
Which sites are affected by the conditioning at
(aN,bN)
?
How does this conditioning influence the law of large numbers and the fluctuation behavior of the last passage time field?

This question has been considered recently for several models.
The conditional law of large numbers was obtained for the KPZ equation in
[
11
]
and for the directed landscape in
[
5
]
.
These works also considered conditioning at multiple points.
Conditional fluctuations were obtained for the KPZ fixed point in
[
13
,
16
,
14
]
and for the periodic KPZ fixed point in
[
1
]
.
In this paper, we examine a different model—the exponential LPP—and prove conditional law of large numbers and conditional fluctuation results.
Regarding the fluctuation results, we extend the works of
[
13
,
16
,
14
,
1
]
to a regime that was not considered before.
Furthermore, we propose several conjectures concerning both the conditional law of large numbers and the limiting fluctuations in the full two-dimensional regime.

For comparison, consider the one-dimensional random field
\mathcal{S}=\{\mathcal{S}_{n}:n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
,
where
\mathcal{S}_{n}=X_{1}+\cdots+X_{n}
is the sum of i.i.d. exponential random variables with mean
\mu=1
.
For
\ell>1
, it is straightforward to show that

{\rm Law}\left(\left\{\frac{\mathcal{S}_{[tN]}-t\ell N}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}(\ell)}}N^{1/2}}\right\}_{t\in(0,1)}\bigg|\mathcal{S}_{N}=\ell N\right)\xrightarrow{\textit{f.d.d.}}{\rm Law}\left(\{\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}(t)\}_{t\in(0,1)}\right)

(1.8)

as
N\to\infty
, where
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}
denotes a standard Brownian bridge, and
\Lambda(\alpha)
is the rate function for the large deviations of the sum of independent exponential random variables.
Explicitly,

\Lambda(\alpha)=\sup_{\lambda}(\alpha\lambda+\log(1-\lambda))=\alpha-1-\log\alpha
for
\alpha>0
, and thus
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Lambda^{\prime\prime}(\ell)}}=\ell
.
See, for example,
[
3
]
for the case where
X_{i}
are general random variables.
Note that
\mathcal{S}
can be viewed as the restriction of the exponential LPP on the first row, since
\mathcal{S}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(n,1)
.

1.2
Conditional law of large numbers

Throughout this paper, the conditional probability
\mathbb{P}(E\,|\,\mathcal{L}(a,b)=c)
is understood as

\mathbb{P}(E\,|\,\mathcal{L}(a,b)=c)=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{P}(E\cap\{\mathcal{L}(a,b)\in(c-\epsilon,c+\epsilon)\})}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(a,b)\in(c-\epsilon,c+\epsilon))}=\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\mathbb{P}(E\cap\{\mathcal{L}(a,b)\leq c\})}{\frac{\partial}{\partial c}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(a,b)\leq c)}.

(1.9)

The first result of this paper is a conditional law of large numbers.
Compare the result with (
1.2
).

Theorem 1.2

(Conditional Law of Large Numbers)
.

Fix
a,b>0
and
\ell>\bar{\mathcal{L}}(a,b)
.
Let
D=\ell^{2}-2(a+b)\ell+(a-b)^{2}
as in (
1.7
) and define the function

h(x,y)=\frac{1}{2}\left[(\ell+a-b)x+(\ell-a+b)y-|x-y|\sqrt{D}\right].

(1.10)

Then, for every
\epsilon>0
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\frac{\mathcal{L}(xaN,ybN)}{N}-h(x,y)\right|>\epsilon\,\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\ell N\right]=0

(1.11)

for
(x,y)\in(0,1)^{2}
satisfying

\frac{1}{\mathrm{m}}<\frac{y}{x}<\mathrm{m}\qquad\text{where}\quad\mathrm{m}:=\frac{\ell-a-b+\sqrt{D}}{\ell-a-b-\sqrt{D}}.

(1.12)

The function
h(x,y)
is a piecewise linear function; see Figure
1
for its level curves.

1
1
x
y

Figure 1
:
Level curves of
h(x,y)

We propose the following conjecture for all points
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}
.

Conjecture 1.3
.

Define the regions

\Omega_{1}=\{(x,y)\in(1,\infty)^{2}:\frac{1}{\mathrm{m}}<\frac{y-1}{x-1}<\mathrm{m}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega_{2}=\{(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}:\frac{1}{\mathrm{m}}<\frac{y}{x}<\mathrm{m}\}.

Under the same assumptions of Theorem
1.2
, we conjecture that (
1.11
) holds with

h(x,y)=\begin{cases}\ell+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{(a,b)}(xa,yb)\quad&\text{for }(x,y)\in\Omega_{1}\\
\frac{1}{2}\left[(\ell+a-b)x+(\ell-a+b)y-|x-y|\sqrt{D}\right]\quad&\text{for }(x,y)\in\Omega_{2}\setminus\Omega_{1}\\
\bar{\mathcal{L}}(xa,yb)\quad&\text{for }(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}\setminus\Omega_{2}.\end{cases}

(1.13)

We provide a heuristic argument for the above conjecture in Subsection
2.1
. See Figure
2
for the picture of the regions
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
.
The conjecture suggests that conditioning on
\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\ell N
does not affect the hydrodynamic limit of the last passage time for points in the region
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}\setminus\Omega_{2}
, and only has a trivial effect on points in
\Omega_{1}
.
In contrast, the hydrodynamic limit of the conditional last passage time to points in
\Omega_{2}\setminus\Omega_{1}
is conjectured to be a piecewise linear function. Theorem
1.2
establishes this part of the conjecture for
(x,y)\in(0,1)^{2}
.

x
y
\Omega_{1}
\Omega_{2}

:
The dark gray region is
\Omega_{1}
. The union of the light gray region and the dark gray region is
\Omega_{2}

The level curves of the conjectured
h(x,y)
are shown on the right panel of Figure
3
.
The function
h(x,y)
is not only continuous, but also
C^{1}
.
At the boundary of
\Omega_{2}
, the level curves of
h(x,y)
are tangential to the level curves of the unconditional limit
\bar{\mathcal{L}}(xa,yb)
.
Similarly, at the boundary of
\Omega_{1}
, the level curves are tangential to the curves
\ell+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{(a,b)}(xa,yb)
.
The left panel displays the level curves of the unconditional limit,
\bar{\mathcal{L}}(xa,yb)
.

:
Left: Level curves of
\bar{\mathcal{L}}(x,y)
for the law of large numbers (
1.2
), rotated by 45 degrees. Right: Level curves of the function
h(x,y)
for the conjectured conditional law of large numbers (
1.13
), rotated by 45 degree, when
a=b=1
and
\ell=5
. The gray area is
\Omega_{1}
; the light gray area denotes
\Omega_{2}\setminus\Omega_{1}
.

As mentioned before, the papers
[
11
]
and
[
5
]
considered the conditional law of large numbers for the KPZ equation and the directed landscape.
These papers state results for times before the conditioning time, which corresponds to the regime
x+y\leq 1
in the exponential LPP.
2
2

2

Li-Cheng Tsai informed us that the result of
[
5
]
can be extended to all times.

1.3
Conditional fluctuations

We now consider fluctuations and present two results. The first pertains to points along the diagonal line, as shown in the left panel of Figure
4
.
For these points, convergence holds in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions. This result may be compared with (
1.4
).

Theorem 1.4

(Diagonal multi-point fluctuations)
.

Fix
a,b>0
and
\ell>\bar{\mathcal{L}}(a,b)
. Let
D=\ell^{2}-(a+b)\ell+(a-b)^{2}
. Define the positive real numbers

\mathsf{\sigma}=\frac{\sqrt{(a+b)\ell-(a-b)^{2}}D^{1/4}}{2\sqrt{ab}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{c}_{\pm}=\bigg(1\pm\frac{(a-b)\sqrt{D}}{(a+b)\ell-(a-b)^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}.

(1.14)

Then,

\begin{split}&{\rm Law}\left(\left\{\frac{\mathcal{L}(taN+s\frac{a(\ell-a+b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}N^{1/2},tbN-s\frac{b(\ell+a-b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}N^{1/2})-t\ell N}{\mathsf{\sigma}N^{1/2}}\right\}_{(s,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times(0,1)}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\ell N\right)\\
&\xrightarrow{\textit{f.d.d.}}{\rm Law}\left(\{\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t)-|\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t)-s|\}_{(s,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times(0,1)}\right)\end{split}

(1.15)

as
N\to\infty
, where

\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t)=\frac{\mathsf{c}_{+}\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{+}(t)+\mathsf{c}_{-}\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{-}(t)}{\sqrt{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t)=\frac{\mathsf{c}_{+}\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{+}(t)-\mathsf{c}_{-}\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{-}(t)}{\sqrt{2}}

(1.16)

for two independent standard Brownian bridges
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{+}
and
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{-}
.

We note that
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}
and
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}
are standard Brownian bridges with covariance

\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t)\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t)]=\frac{(a-b)\sqrt{D}}{(a+b)\ell-(a-b)^{2}}t(1-t),\qquad t\in(0,1).

(1.17)

They are independent only when
a=b
.

Figure 4
:
The left picture is related to Theorem
1.4
. The right picture is related to Theorem
1.5
.

The second result concerns fluctuations at points off the diagonal line, as shown in the right panel of Figure
4
. However, for these points, we were only able to prove convergence for two-point distributions.

Theorem 1.5

(Off-diagonal two-point fluctuations)
.

Fix
a,b>0
and
\ell>\bar{\mathcal{L}}(a,b)
.
Let the function
h(x,y)
and the positive numbers
\mathrm{m}
,
\mathsf{\sigma}
, and
\mathsf{c}_{\pm}
be as defined in Theorems
1.2
and
1.4
.
Then, for two distinct points
(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\in(0,1)^{2}
and two real numbers
\mathrm{r}_{1},\mathrm{r}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\begin{split}&\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[\frac{\mathcal{L}(x_{i}aN,y_{i}bN)-h(x_{i},y_{i})N}{\sqrt{2}\mathsf{\sigma}N^{1/2}}>\mathrm{r}_{i},\ i=1,2\bigg|\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\ell N\right]\\
&\qquad\quad=\begin{cases}\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{c}_{+}\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{m}y_{i}-x_{i}}{\mathrm{m}-1}\right)>\mathrm{r}_{i},i=1,2\right]\qquad&\text{if $\frac{1}{\mathrm{m}}<\frac{y_{1}}{x_{1}},\frac{y_{2}}{x_{2}}<1$,}\\
\mathbb{P}\left[\mathsf{c}_{-}\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{m}x_{i}-y_{i}}{\mathrm{m}-1}\right)>\mathrm{r}_{i},i=1,2\right]\qquad&\text{if $1<\frac{y_{1}}{x_{1}},\frac{y_{2}}{x_{2}}<\mathrm{m}$,}\end{cases}\end{split}

where
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}
is a standard Brownian bridge, and
\mathrm{m}
is the constant defined in (
1.12
).

We expect that the results will hold for multi-point distributions as well. However, since the analysis becomes quite involved, in this paper we focus only on two-point distribution results, leaving the general case for future work.

1
1
x
y
1
1
x
y

Figure 5
:
Left: Level curves of
(x,y)\mapsto\mathrm{m}y-x
for
y<x
. Right: Level curves of
(x,y)\mapsto\mathrm{m}x-y
for
y>x

The above result shows that if two points in the gray region in the right panel of Figure
4
lie on a level curve of the mapping
(x,y)\mapsto\mathrm{m}y-x
and both are below the diagonal line, then the corresponding limiting two-point distributions are identical. A similar statement holds for the mapping
(x,y)\mapsto\mathrm{m}x-y
for points above the diagonal line; see Figure
5
.
We further conjecture that the distributions for points below the diagonal line and those for points above the diagonal line become independent. In other words, the two Brownian bridges appearing in Theorem
1.5
are independent; see below.

For general points, we propose the following conjecture. A heuristic argument supporting this conjecture is provided in Subsection
2.2
.
Although items (a) and (c) below are stated only for one-point distributions, the extension of the conjecture to convergence to the KPZ fixed point—analogous to (
1.4
)—is straightforward, and thus we omit it here.

Conjecture 1.6
.

Let
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
be the regions defined in Conjecture
1.3
, and let
h(x,y)
be the function defined in (
1.13
).
Under the same assumptions and notation as in Theorem
1.4
and
1.5
, and conditional on the event
\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\ell N
, we conjecture that the following results hold.

1.

For each
(x,y)\in\Omega_{1}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{L}(xaN,ybN)-h(x,y)N}{(ab(x-1)(y-1))^{-1/6}(\sqrt{a(x-1)}+\sqrt{b(y-1)})^{4/3}N^{1/3}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\TW_{2}.

(1.18)

2.

We expect that

\frac{\mathcal{L}(xaN,ybN)-h(x,y)N}{\sqrt{2}\mathsf{\sigma}N^{1/2}}\xrightarrow{\textit{f.d.d.}}\begin{cases}\mathsf{c}_{+}\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{+}\left(\frac{\mathrm{m}y-x}{\mathrm{m}-1}\right)\quad&\text{for $(x,y)\in\Omega_{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}_{1}$ satisfying $y<x$,}\\
\mathsf{c}_{-}\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{-}\left(\frac{\mathrm{m}x-y}{\mathrm{m}-1}\right)\quad&\text{for $(x,y)\in\Omega_{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}_{1}$ satisfying $y>x$}\end{cases}

(1.19)

as
N\to\infty
, where
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{+}
and
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{-}
are independent standard Brownian bridges, and furthermore, they are the same ones appearing in (
1.16
).

3.

For each
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}_{2}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{L}(xaN,ybN)-h(x,y)N}{(abxy)^{-1/6}(\sqrt{ax}+\sqrt{by})^{4/3}N^{1/3}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\TW_{2}.

(1.20)

1.4
Comparison with the conditional KPZ fixed point

The KPZ fixed point under a one-point upper large deviation event was recently studied in
[
13
,
16
,
14
]
.
Let
\mathsf{H}_{\textnormal{step}}(s,t)
for
(s,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, denote the KPZ fixed point with the narrow wedge initial condition.
In
[
13
, Remark1.5]
, Liu and Wang proved that
3
3

3

We have rewritten the result of
[
13
]
using the identity
\min\{u,v\}=\frac{u+v-|u-v|}{2}
, and adjusted the parameters to eliminate the factor
\sqrt{2}
.
for every
(X,T)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,

\begin{split}&{\rm Law}\left(\left\{\frac{\mathsf{H}_{\textnormal{step}}(tX+s\frac{T^{3/4}}{L^{1/4}},tT)-tL}{2T^{1/4}L^{1/4}}\right\}_{(s,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times(0,1)}\,\bigg|\,\mathsf{H}_{\textnormal{step}}(X,T)=L\right)\\
&\qquad\xrightarrow{\textit{f.d.d.}}{\rm Law}\left(\{\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t)-|\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t)-s|\}_{(s,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times(0,1)}\right)\end{split}

(1.21)

as
L\to\infty
, where the Brownian bridges
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}
and
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}
are independent.
Theorem
1.4
is similar to this result, but the Brownian bridges that appear in that theorem are generally not independent. It is intriguing why this dependence arises in the exponential LPP. While this dependence follows from explicit computation, we do not have a simple conceptual explanation for this phenomenon.

In Theorem
1.5
, we established the conditional fluctuation theorem for points off the diagonal line. A similar result has not yet been obtained for the KPZ fixed point.

The fluctuations of the conditional KPZ fixed point near the conditioning time have been studied in
[
14
]
, and after the conditioning time in
[
16
]
. A version of Theorem
1.4
was similarly established for the periodic KPZ fixed point in
[
1
]
; in that context, the limiting distribution involves a Brownian bridge and a Brownian bridge on a circle, which are again independent.

1.5
Method of proof and outline of the paper

Theorem
1.2
follows from Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
; thus, we prove only these latter two theorems.
Our approach is based on the analysis of explicit multi-point distribution formulas for the exponential LPP. The multi-point distributions in so-called space-like directions were computed in the mid-2000s in
[
9
,
2
]
. The distributions for general points, including those in time-like directions, were obtained more recently by Liu
[
12
]
.

The proof of Theorem
1.4
is similar to that of
[
13
]
for the KPZ fixed point, and we have adapted it for the exponential LPP.
However, the proof of Theorem
1.5
requires substantially more effort and constitutes the most technical part of this paper.

The explicit multi-point distribution formula from
[
12
]
involves an integral of a Fredholm determinant. In random matrix theory and KPZ models, upper large deviation and upper tail limits are often readily obtained from Fredholm determinants, as the operator becomes small and the determinant can be approximated by its trace using the method of steepest descent. In our case, however, the operator acts on nested contours.
For Theorem
1.5
, the critical points relevant to the steepest descent method are ordered such that the contours cannot be deformed appropriately without crossing the poles of the kernel. As a consequence, we must keep track of all residue contributions, which quickly becomes challenging.

Due to these complexities, we restrict our analysis to two-point distribution results and leave multi-point distribution considerations for future work. Even for two-point distributions, the locations of the critical points depend on the relative positions of the points, requiring the consideration of seven distinct regimes. In contrast, the proof of Theorem
1.4
is simpler, since the critical points are fixed and the poles of the kernel do not need to be considered.

Although we do not use the Fredholm determinant formula directly in our analysis, instead relying on its series expansion, we still encounter the same underlying challenges.

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we provide heuristic reasoning behind Conjectures
1.3
and
1.6
, and state an additional conjecture regarding conditional geodesics.
In Section
3
, we present explicit formulas for the multi-time conditional distributions.
Section
4
contains two miscellaneous lemmas used throughout the paper.
In Section
5
, we examine functions that play a central role in our analysis and derive their limits and bounds.
The proof of Theorem
1.4
is given in Section
6
.
Finally, Theorem
1.5
, which constitutes the most technical part of the paper, is proved in Section
7
.

2
Heuristics

We give a heuristic argument for Conjectures
1.3
and
1.6
.
We also discuss a conjecture on the conditional geodesics.

2.1
Heuristic argument for Conjecture
1.3

ta
xa
a
tb
yb
b

Figure 6
:
Conjectural maximizing path

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\ell N
for
\ell>\bar{\mathcal{L}}(a,b)
.
For
\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)
to be large, it suffices for a single path
\pi
ending at the point
(aN,bN)
to have a large value of
E(\pi)=\sum_{\mathbf{v}\in\pi}\omega_{\mathbf{v}}
.
It is reasonable to expect that such a path is close to the straight line from
(0,0)
to
(aN,bN)
.
If, moreover, we assume that the values of
\omega_{\mathbf{v}}
along this path are roughly of the similar order (i.e., the large value of
E(\pi)
is not due to a small number of exceptional sites
{\mathbf{v}}
), then we may expect that
\mathcal{L}(taN,tbN)\approx t\ell N
for every
t\in(0,1)
.
For general points
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}
, considering a path that is approximately a straight line from
(1,1)
to
(taN,tbN)
for some
t\in[0,1]
, followed by an approximately straight line from
(taN,tbN)
to
(xaN,ybN)
(see Figure
6
), we conjecture that conditional on the event
\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\ell N
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{L}(xaN,ybN)}{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{=}}\max\left\{H(t):0\leq t\leq\min\{x,y,1\}\right\}\quad\text{where}\quad H(t)=t\ell+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{ta,tb}(xa,yb).

(2.1)

Here, the condition
t\leq\min\{x,y,1\}
ensures that the line segment from
(taN,tbN)
to
(xaN,ybN)
has a non-negative slope.
We find that the maximizer is

t_{c}=\begin{cases}1\qquad&\text{for $(x,y)\in\Omega_{1}$,}\\
\frac{\mathrm{m}y-x}{\mathrm{m}-1}\qquad&\text{for $(x,y)\in\Omega_{2}\setminus\Omega_{1}$ satisfying $y<x$,}\\
\frac{\mathrm{m}x-y}{\mathrm{m}-1}\qquad&\text{for $(x,y)\in\Omega_{2}\setminus\Omega_{1}$ satisfying $y>x$,}\\
0\qquad&\text{for $(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}\setminus\Omega_{2}$,}\\
\end{cases}

(2.2)

and the maximum value is

H(t_{c})=t_{c}\ell+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{t_{c}a,t_{c}b}(xa,yb)=h(x,y)

(2.3)

as in (
1.13
).

For the directed landscape, the results of
[
4
]
, especially Proposition 2.1, show that the heuristic argument above essentially holds in that model.

2.2
Heuristic argument for Conjecture
1.6

x
1
y
1
x
1
y
1
x
1
y
1

Figure 7
:
Conjectural maximizing path for the fluctuations

We assume that Theorem
1.4
has already been established, and now argue for the conjecture.
Suppose that
\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\ell N
for some
\ell>\bar{\mathcal{L}}(a,b)
, and let
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}\setminus\{(t,t):0<t\leq 1\}
.
We follow the same reasoning as in Conjecture
1.3
, but now include the next-order asymptotic terms.
Additionally, we consider more general “mid-way” points.
In the previous subsection, the mid-way points were
t(aN,bN)
,
t\in(0,1)
; see Figure
6
.
This time, we consider the points
\mathbf{p}^{s}_{t}N\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}
, where

\mathbf{p}^{s}_{t}=t(a,b)+s(c_{1}N^{-1/2},-c_{2}N^{-1/2})\quad\text{with $c_{1}=\frac{a(\ell-a+b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}$ and $c_{2}=\frac{b(\ell+a-b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}$}

for some
(t,s)\in(0,1)\times{\mathbb{R}}
. See the leftmost panel in Figure
7
.
As in the last subsection, we again expect that
t=t_{c}
:

\mathcal{L}(xaN,ybN)\approx\max\{\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{p}^{s}_{t_{c}}N)+\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{p}^{s}_{t_{c}}N}(xaN,ybN):s\in{\mathbb{R}},\,\mathbf{p}^{s}_{t_{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}\}.

From Theorem
1.4
,

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{p}^{s}_{t_{c}}N)\approx t_{c}\ell N+\mathsf{\sigma}(\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t_{c})-|\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t_{c})-s|)N^{1/2}.

On the other hand, using the unconditional fluctuation result (
1.3
), we expect that

\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{p}^{s}_{t_{c}}N}(xaN,ybN)\approx\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbf{p}^{s}_{t_{c}}}(xa,yb)N+\frac{(\sqrt{a(x-t_{c})}+\sqrt{b(y-t_{c})})^{4/3}}{(a(x-t_{c})b(y-t_{c}))^{1/6}}\TW_{2}N^{1/3}.

Using Taylor’s theorem, we see that

\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbf{p}^{s}_{t_{c}}}(xa,yb)=\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{(t_{c}a,t_{c}b)}(xa,yb)+\mathsf{\sigma}sR(t_{c})N^{-1/2}+O(N^{-1})

where

R(t)=Q\left(\frac{y-t}{x-t}\right),\qquad Q(u)=\frac{\sqrt{ab}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}\left[\frac{\ell+a-b}{\sqrt{u}}-(\ell-a+b)\sqrt{u}-\frac{(a-b)(\ell-a-b)}{\sqrt{ab}}\right].

(2.4)

Since
t_{c}\ell+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{(t_{c}a,t_{c}b)}(xa,yb)=h(x,y)
from (
2.3
), we are thus led to conjecture that

\mathcal{L}(xaN,ybN)\approx h(x,y)N+\mathsf{\sigma}ZN^{1/2}+\frac{(\sqrt{a(x-t_{c})}+\sqrt{b(y-t_{c})})^{4/3}}{(ab(x-t_{c})(y-t_{c}))^{1/6}}\TW_{2}N^{1/3}

where

Z=\max\{r(s):s\in{\mathbb{R}},\,\mathbf{p}^{s}_{t_{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}\},\qquad r(s):=\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t_{c})-|\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t_{c})-s|+R(t_{c})s.

We now evaluate
Z
.
Observe that
Q(u)
in (
2.4
) is a monotonically decreasing function of
u>0
. Also, note from the formula
\mathrm{m}=\frac{\ell-a-b+\sqrt{D}}{\ell-a-b-\sqrt{D}}
that
Q(\frac{1}{\mathrm{m}})=1
and
Q(\mathrm{m})=-1
.

•

Suppose
(x,y)\in\Omega_{1}
. In this case, we have
t_{c}=1
from (
2.2
) and thus
r(s)=-|s|+R(1)s
. See the rightmost panel in Figure
7
. Since the condition
(x,y)\in\Omega_{1}
implies that
\frac{1}{\mathrm{m}}<\frac{y-1}{x-1}<\mathrm{m}
, we find that

R(1)=Q(\frac{y-1}{x-1})\in[Q(\mathrm{m}),Q(\frac{1}{\mathrm{m}})]=[-1,1]
by the monotonicity of the function
Q
.
Hence,
|R(1)|\leq 1
, and thus, the maximum of
r(s)=-|s|+R(1)s
is
r(0)=0
. Therefore,
Z=0
and

\mathcal{L}(xaN,ybN)\approx h(x,y)N+\frac{(\sqrt{a(x-1)}+\sqrt{b(y-1)})^{4/3}}{(ab(x-1)(y-1))^{1/6}}\TW_{2}N^{1/3}
. This is (
1.18
).

•

Suppose
(x,y)\in\Omega_{2}\setminus\overline{\Omega}_{1}
.
In this case,
t_{c}=\frac{\mathrm{m}y-x}{\mathrm{m}-1}
if
y<x
, and
t_{c}=\frac{\mathrm{m}x-y}{\mathrm{m}-1}
if
y>x
.
See the leftmost panel in Figure
7
.
Thus,
R(t_{c})=Q(\frac{1}{\mathrm{m}})=1
if
y<x
, and
R(t_{c})=Q(\mathrm{m})=-1
if
y>x
.
Hence,
r(s)=\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t_{c})-|\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t_{c})-s|\pm s
, with the sign
+
for
y<x
and
-
for
y>x
.
The maximum occurs at
s=\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t_{c})
, yielding

Z=\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t_{c})+\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t_{c})
if
y<x
, and
Z=\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t_{c})-\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t_{c})
if
y>x
.
Thus, using from (
1.16
), we find that
\mathcal{L}(xaN,ubN)\approx h(x,y)N+\mathsf{\sigma}ZN^{1/2}
with
Z=\sqrt{2}\mathsf{c}_{\pm}\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{\pm}(t_{c})
as in (
1.19
).

•

Suppose
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\setminus\overline{\Omega}_{2}
. In this case, we have
t_{c}=0
. See the middle panel in Figure
7
.
Since
\mathbf{p}^{s}_{0}=s(c_{1},-c_{2})N^{-1/2}
lies in
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}
only for
s=0
, we find that
Z=0
. Therefore,

\mathcal{L}(xaN,ubN)\approx h(x,y)N+\frac{(\sqrt{ax}+\sqrt{by})^{4/3}}{(abxy)^{1/6}}\TW_{2}N^{1/3}
.
This corresponds to (
1.20
).

This completes our heuristic argument for Conjecture
1.6
.

2.3
Conjecture on conditional geodesics

The diagonal fluctuation result, Theorem
1.4
, suggests a conjecture regarding the geodesic.
The following consideration is analogous to that in
[
13
, Conjecture 1.11]
for the conditional KPZ fixed point.

Let
\pi_{*}
be the geodesic from
(1,1)
to the site
(aN,bN)
, i.e.,

\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\max_{\pi\in(1,1)\to(aN,bN)}E(\pi)=E(\pi_{*}).

Since
\omega_{\mathbf{v}}
are continuous random variables, the geodesic is unique almost surely. The path
\pi_{*}
is a sequence of points in
{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
. We linearly interpolate so that it becomes a collection of a line segments. Using the basis vectors

\mathbf{v}_{1}=(a,b),\qquad\mathbf{v}_{2}=\left(\frac{a(\ell-a+b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}},-\frac{b(\ell+a-b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}\right)

we may write

\pi_{*}=\{\tau\mathbf{v}_{1}+\pi^{*}(\tau)\mathbf{v}_{2}\}_{\tau\in[0,N]}

for a function
\pi^{*}(\tau)
,
\tau\in[0,N]
, satisfying
\pi^{*}(0)=\pi^{*}(N)=0
.
By the geometry of the geodesic, this function is well-defined.

Now, assume
\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\ell N
and consider the geodesic to
(aN,bN)
.
From the limit in Theorem
1.4
,
we observe that the function
x\mapsto\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t)-|\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t)-x|
achieves its maximum at
x=\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t)
with maximum value
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t)
.
This observation leads us to the following conjecture.

Conjecture 2.1
.

Using the same notation as in Theorem
1.4
, we conjecture that

\begin{split}&{\rm Law}\left(\left(\frac{\pi^{*}(tN)}{N^{1/2}},\frac{\mathcal{L}\left(tN\mathbf{v}_{1}+\pi^{*}(tN)\mathbf{v}_{2}\right)-t\ell N}{\mathsf{\sigma}N^{1/2}}\right)_{t\in(0,1)}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{L}(aN,bN)=\ell N\right)\xrightarrow{\textit{f.d.d.}}{\rm Law}\left((\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t),\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t))_{t\in(0,1)}\right)\end{split}

where
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}
and
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}
are correlated Brownian bridges given by (
1.16
).

For the directed landscape, the convergence of a quantity similar to
\frac{\pi^{*}(tN)}{N^{1/2}}
to a Brownian bridge was proved in
[
6
]
.

3
Conditional multi-point distributions

As mentioned in the Introduction, we prove Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
by computing the limits of an explicit formula for the conditional multi-point distributions.
The exponential LPP is equivalent to the continuous-time totally asymmetric simple exclusion process (TASEP) with step initial condition: for
(M,N)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
and
T\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(M,N)>T)=\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{x}_{N}(T)<M-N)

(3.1)

where
\mathsf{x}_{k}(T)
denotes the position of the
k^{\text{th}}
particle in the TASEP at time
T
.
In
[
12
]
, Liu obtained an explicit formula for multi-time distributions for the TASEP.
Using the relation (
3.1
), the case
I=\{1,\cdots,m-1\}
in Proposition 2.3 of
[
12
]
, specialized to the step initial condition, gives a formula for the probabilities

{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{L}(M_{1},N_{1})>T_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{L}(M_{m-1},N_{m-1})>T_{m-1},\mathcal{L}(M_{m},N_{m})\leq T_{m})

of the exponential LPP.
We can thus find a formula for the multi-point conditional distributions by computing

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{L}(M_{1},N_{1})>T_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{L}(M_{m-1},N_{m-1})>T_{m-1}|\mathcal{L}(M_{m},N_{m})=T_{m})\\
&=\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial T_{m}}{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{L}(M_{1},N_{1})>T_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{L}(M_{m-1},N_{m-1})>T_{m-1},\mathcal{L}(M_{m},N_{m})\leq T_{m})}{\frac{\partial}{\partial T_{m}}{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{L}(M_{m},N_{m})\leq T_{m})}\end{split}

(3.2)

In this section, we state explicit formulas for multi-point conditional distributions.
We begin by introducing several notations in Subsection
3.1
. The main formula is presented in Proposition
3.1
in Subsection
3.2
.
A few special cases of the formula are discussed in Subsection
3.3
.
Throughout this section, we fix a positive integer
m
.

3.1
Definitions

Let

\mathrm{K}_{n}(\mathbf{r}|\mathbf{s})=\det\left[\frac{1}{r_{i}-s_{j}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{n}=\frac{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(r_{i}-r_{j})(s_{j}-s_{i})}{\prod_{i,j=1}^{n}(r_{i}-s_{j})}

(3.3)

be the Cauchy determinant for the vectors
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{n})
and
\mathbf{s}=(s_{1},\cdots,s_{n})
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
.
Define

\mathrm{S}_{n}(\mathbf{r}|\mathbf{s})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(r_{i}-s_{i}).

(3.4)

We often suppress the subscript
n
if the sizes of the vectors are clear from context, and simply write
\mathrm{K}(\mathbf{r}|\mathbf{s})
and
\mathrm{S}(\mathbf{r}|\mathbf{s})
instead.
For
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\cdots,n_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
, define the rational function

\begin{split}\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})=&\mathrm{K}_{n_{1}}(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{1}|\boldsymbol{\xi}^{1})\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathrm{K}_{n_{i}+n_{i+1}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{i},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{i+1}|\boldsymbol{\eta}^{i},\boldsymbol{\xi}^{i+1})\right]\mathrm{K}_{n_{m}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{m}|\boldsymbol{\eta}^{m})\mathrm{S}_{n_{m}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{m}|\boldsymbol{\eta}^{m})\end{split}

(3.5)

where
\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{m})
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{m})

with
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{i},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{i}}
.
When
m=1
, the above formula becomes, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\Pi_{n}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}):=\mathrm{K}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\eta}|\boldsymbol{\xi})\mathrm{K}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\xi}|\boldsymbol{\eta})\mathrm{S}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\xi}|\boldsymbol{\eta})\qquad\text{for $\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n}$.}

For
M,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, define the function
5
5

5

Throughout the paper
\log
denotes the branch of the logarithm function that is analytic in
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\mathrm{i}\overline{{\mathbb{R}}_{-}}
and satisfies
\log 1=0
.

f_{M,N,T}(z)=\frac{z^{N}e^{Tz}}{(z+1)^{M}}=e^{N\log z-M\log(z+1)+Tz}.

(3.6)

For
\mathbf{M}=(M_{1},\cdots,M_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
,
\mathbf{N}=(N_{1},\cdots,N_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
,
\mathbf{T}=(T_{1},\cdots,T_{m})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{m}
, and
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\cdots,n_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
, define the function

{\mathsf{F}}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{k_{i}=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{{\mathsf{f}}_{i}(\xi_{k_{i}}^{i})}{{\mathsf{f}}_{i}(\eta_{k_{i}}^{i})},\qquad{\mathsf{f}}_{i}(z)=\frac{f_{M_{i},N_{i},T_{i}}(z)}{f_{M_{i-1},N_{i-1},T_{i-1}}(z)}

(3.7)

where
\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\xi}^{m})
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(\boldsymbol{\eta}^{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{m})
as before, with
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{i}=(\xi^{i}_{1},\cdots,\xi^{i}_{n_{i}})
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{i}=(\eta^{i}_{1},\cdots,\eta^{i}_{n_{i}})
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n_{i}}
.
In the above formula, we set
M_{0}=N_{0}=T_{0}=0
.

Let

C^{\text{in}}_{m,\text{left}},\cdots,C^{\text{in}}_{2,\text{left}},C_{1,\text{left}},C^{\text{out}}_{2,\text{left}},\cdots,C^{\text{out}}_{m,\text{left}}

be
2m-1
small circles, nested from inside to outside, that enclose the point
-1
. Similarly, let

C^{\text{in}}_{m,\text{right}},\cdots,C^{\text{in}}_{2,\text{right}},C_{1,\text{right}},C^{\text{out}}_{2,\text{right}},\cdots,C^{\text{out}}_{m,\text{right}}

be
2m-1
small circles, also nested from inside to outside, that enclose the point
0
and are disjoint from the previous circles.
See Figure
8
for the case when
m=2
.
The circles are oriented counter-clockwise.
6
6

6

All closed contours in this paper are oriented counterclockwise, unless otherwise specified. The orientations of infinite contours will be stated explicitly.

-1
0

Figure 8
:
Contours for
m=2
: The three circles on the left are
C^{\text{in}}_{2,\text{left}}
,
C_{1,\text{left}}
,
C^{\text{out}}_{2,\text{left}}
listed from inside to outside. The three circles on the right are
C^{\text{in}}_{2,\text{right}}
,
C_{1,\text{right}}
,
C^{\text{out}}_{2,\text{right}}
, also listed from inside to outside.

For
\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
,
\mathbf{M}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
,
\mathbf{N}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
, and
\mathbf{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{m}
,
define the polynomial
\mathsf{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{z})
in
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{m-1})
of degree
2|\mathbf{n}|-2n_{1}
by

\begin{split}\mathsf{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{z})=&\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2|\mathbf{n}|}}\prod_{i=2}^{m}\prod_{k_{i}=1}^{n_{i}}\left[\int_{C_{i,\textnormal{left}}^{\textnormal{in}}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{k_{i}}^{i}+z_{i-1}\int_{C_{i,\textnormal{left}}^{\textnormal{out}}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{k_{i}}^{i}\right]\left[\int_{C_{i,\textnormal{right}}^{\textnormal{in}}}\mathrm{d}\eta_{k_{i}}^{i}+z_{i-1}\int_{C_{i,\textnormal{right}}^{\textnormal{out}}}\mathrm{d}\eta_{k_{i}}^{i}\right]\\
&\times\prod_{k_{1}=1}^{n_{1}}\left[\int_{C_{1,\textnormal{left}}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{k_{1}}^{1}\right]\left[\int_{C_{1,\textnormal{right}}}\mathrm{d}\eta_{k_{1}}^{1}\right]\,\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}){\mathsf{F}}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})\end{split}

(3.8)

where

|\mathbf{n}|=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{m}\qquad\text{for $\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\cdots,n_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}$.}

The coefficients of this polynomial are linear combinations of
2|\mathbf{n}|
-fold contour integrals.
Note that when
m=1
,
\mathsf{D}^{(n)}_{M,N,T}
is a constant.

3.2
Formula for conditional multi-point distributions

We now state an explicit formula for the conditional multi-point distributions.
The formula is similar to that for the KPZ fixed point studied in
[
13
, Lemma 2.2 and Lemma 3.1]
, and the proof is also nearly identical, since the multi-point distribution formulas share similar structures.

Proposition 3.1
.

Consider the exponential LPP in Definition
1.1
. Fix an integer
m\geq 2
.
Let
\mathbf{M}=(M_{1},\cdots,M_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
,
\mathbf{N}=(N_{1},\cdots,N_{m})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
, and
\mathbf{T}=(T_{1},\cdots,T_{m})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{m}
.
Assume that
0<T_{1}\leq\cdots\leq T_{m}
and
(N_{1},T_{1}),\cdots,(N_{m},T_{m})
are all distinct.
Then,

{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{L}(M_{1},N_{1})>T_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{L}(M_{m-1},N_{m-1})>T_{m-1}|\mathcal{L}(M_{m},N_{m})=T_{m})=\frac{\mathsf{Q}_{m}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T})}{\mathsf{Q}_{1}(M_{m},N_{m},T_{m})}

(3.9)

where

\mathsf{Q}_{m}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T})=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}}\frac{1}{(\mathbf{n}!)^{2}}\mathsf{Q}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{m}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T})

(3.10)

with

\begin{split}\mathsf{Q}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{m}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T})=\frac{(-1)^{|\mathbf{n}|+m-1}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{m-1}}\oint_{>1}\cdots\oint_{>1}\mathsf{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{z})\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{(z_{i}+1)^{n_{i}-n_{i+1}-1}}{z_{i}^{n_{i+1}+1}}\mathrm{d}z_{i}.\end{split}

(3.11)

The function
\mathsf{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{z})
is defined in (
3.8
), and the contours are circles centered at the origin with radii greater than
1
.

Proof.

Recall the notation
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:=\{0\}\cup{\mathbb{N}}
.
Using the relation (
3.1
), the formula for multi-point distributions for TASEP in
[
12
, Proposition 2.3]
, specialized to the case
I=\{1,\cdots,m-1\}
, implies that
7
7

7

We have replaced
z_{i}
with
-z_{i}
in the formula (3) of
[
12
]
.

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{L}(M_{1},N_{1})>T_{1},...,\mathcal{L}(M_{m-1},N_{m-1})>T_{m-1},\mathcal{L}(M_{m},N_{m})\leq T_{m})\\
&=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{m-1}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{m}}\frac{(-1)^{|\mathbf{n}|+m-1}}{(\mathbf{n}!)^{2}}\oint_{>1}\cdots\oint_{>1}\widetilde{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{z})\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{(z_{i}+1)^{n_{i}-n_{i+1}-1}}{z_{i}^{n_{i+1}+1}}\mathrm{d}z_{i}\end{split}

(3.12)

where
\widetilde{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{z})
is
8
8

8

We use different conventions from
[
12
]
. By carefully accounting for the measures in Proposition 2.10 of
[
12
]
and using the Cauchy determinant formula (
3.3
), we find that
\mathsf{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(z_{1},\cdots,z_{m-1})
is equal to
(-1)^{|\mathbf{n}|}\mathcal{D}_{\boldsymbol{n},{Y_{\mathrm{step}}}}(-z_{1},\cdots,-z_{m-1})\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{z_{j}^{n_{j+1}}}{(1+z_{j})^{n_{j}-n_{j+1}}}
in terms of the notation in Section 2.1.3.2 of
[
12
]
.

the same as
\mathsf{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{z})
, except that the term
\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})
is replaced by
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\mathbf{n}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})
, which is given by (
3.5
) without the factor
\mathrm{S}(\boldsymbol{\xi}^{m}|\boldsymbol{\eta}^{m})
.
The assumptions that
0<T_{1}\leq\cdots\leq T_{m}
and that the pairs
(N_{1},T_{1}),\cdots,(N_{m},T_{m})
are all distinct are necessary since
[
12
, Proposition 2.3]
requires similar conditions.

We insert the above formula into equation (
3.2
).
Noting that
T_{m}
appears only in the function
f_{M_{m},N_{m},T_{m}}
, we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial T_{m}}\widetilde{\Pi}_{\mathbf{n}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}){\mathsf{F}}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})=\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}){\mathsf{F}}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}).

Thus, we arrive at the formula (
3.9
), but with the series in both the numerator and denominator taken over
\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{m}
.

Now Lemma
3.2
below implies that
\mathsf{Q}_{m}^{(\mathbf{n})}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T})=0
if
\mathbf{n}\in({\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{m-1}\setminus{\mathbb{N}}^{m-1})\times{\mathbb{N}}
.
Furthermore, since
\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})=0
for
\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{m-1}\times\{0\}
by formula (
3.5
),
we find that
\mathsf{Q}^{(\mathbf{n})}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T})=0
for
\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{m}\setminus{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
.
Therefore, the series over
\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{m}
reduces to a series over
\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}
.
The series for the denominator is similar.
∎

The following lemma is used in the proof of the above proposition.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
H_{\mathbf{n}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})
be an integrable function that does not depend on
\mathbf{z}
. Then, the function

\begin{split}G^{(\mathbf{n})}(\mathbf{z})=&\prod_{i=2}^{m}\prod_{k_{i}=1}^{n_{i}}\left[\int_{C_{i,\textnormal{left}}^{\textnormal{in}}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{k_{i}}^{i}+z_{i-1}\int_{C_{i,\textnormal{left}}^{\textnormal{out}}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{k_{i}}^{i}\right]\left[\int_{C_{i,\textnormal{right}}^{\textnormal{in}}}\mathrm{d}\eta_{k_{i}}^{i}+z_{i-1}\int_{C_{i,\textnormal{right}}^{\textnormal{out}}}\mathrm{d}\eta_{k_{i}}^{i}\right]H_{\mathbf{n}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})\end{split}

satisfies

\oint_{>1}\cdots\oint_{>1}G^{(\mathbf{n})}(\mathbf{z})\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{(z_{i}+1)^{n_{i}-n_{i+1}-1}}{z_{i}^{n_{i+1}+1}}\mathrm{d}z_{i}=0\qquad\text{for $\mathbf{n}\in({\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{m-1}\setminus{\mathbb{N}}^{m-1})\times{\mathbb{N}}$.}

Proof.

Note that
G^{(\mathbf{n})}(\mathbf{z})
is a polynomial of degree
2n_{i+1}
in each variable
z_{i}
for
i=1,\cdots,m-1
.
If
\mathbf{n}\in({\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{m-1}\setminus{\mathbb{N}}^{m-1})\times{\mathbb{N}}
, then there exists
i\in\{1,\cdots,m-1\}
such that
n_{i}=0
and
n_{i+1}\geq 1
. In this case,
G^{(\mathbf{n})}(\mathbf{z})=O(z_{i}^{2n_{i+1}})
and
\frac{(z_{i}+1)^{n_{i}-n_{i+1}-1}}{z_{i}^{n_{i+1}+1}}=O(z_{i}^{-2n_{i+1}-2})
as
z_{i}\to\infty
.
Therefore, the integrand decays sufficiently fast at infinity, and the result follows from Cauchy’s theorem.
∎

3.3
Formulas for two special cases

The case where
\mathbf{n}=(1,\cdots,1)=:\mathbf{1}
will play a special role. The formula (
3.11
) simplifies in this case.

Lemma 3.3
.

We have

\mathsf{Q}^{(\mathbf{1})}_{m}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T})=-\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2m}}\int_{\vec{\gamma}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}\int_{\vec{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\eta}\,\,\Pi_{\mathbf{1}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}){\mathsf{F}}^{(\mathbf{1})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})

(3.13)

where
\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi^{1},\cdots,\xi^{m})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(\eta^{1},\cdots,\eta^{m})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
. The contours are

\vec{\gamma}=\gamma_{1}\times\cdots\times\gamma_{m},\qquad\vec{\Gamma}=\Gamma_{1}\times\cdots\times\Gamma_{m},

(3.14)

where
\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{m}
are small circles around the point
z=-1
, nested from inside to outside, and
\Gamma_{1},\cdots,\Gamma_{m}
are small circles around the point
z=0
, also nested from inside to outside, such that all circles are mutually disjoint.

Proof.

When
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{1}
, the formula (
3.11
) becomes

\begin{split}\mathsf{Q}^{(\mathbf{1})}_{m}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T})=-\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{m-1}}\oint_{>1}\cdots\oint_{>1}\mathsf{D}^{(\mathbf{1})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{z})\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{z_{i}^{2}(z_{i}+1)}\mathrm{d}z_{i}.\end{split}

The function
\mathsf{D}^{(\mathbf{1})}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\mathbf{z})
is a polynomial of degree
2
in each
z_{i}
for
i=1,\cdots,m-1
.
Thus, the
z_{i}
-integrals retain only the leading coefficients of the polynomial, which effectively removes all
C_{i,\textnormal{left}}^{\textnormal{in}}
- and
C_{i,\textnormal{right}}^{\textnormal{in}}
-integrals.
We then relabel the contours as follows:
C_{1,\textnormal{left}}=\gamma_{1}
,
C_{1,\textnormal{right}}=\Gamma_{1}
, and

C_{i,\textnormal{left}}^{\textnormal{out}}=\gamma_{i}
and
C_{i,\textnormal{right}}^{\textnormal{in}}=\Gamma_{i}
for
i=2,\cdots,m
.
The same calculation was also carried out for the KPZ fixed point in
[
13
, Lemma 3.5]
.
∎

For later use, we note that

\Pi_{\mathbf{1}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})=\frac{(-1)^{m}}{\xi^{m}-\eta^{m}}\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{(\xi^{i}-\eta^{i+1})(\eta^{i}-\xi^{i+1})}{(\xi^{i}-\xi^{i+1})(\eta^{i}-\eta^{i+1})(\xi^{i}-\eta^{i})^{2}}.

(3.15)

For the proof of Theorem
1.5
, we also need to evaluate the limit of

\mathsf{Q}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{m}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T})
when
m=3
and
\mathbf{n}=(1,2,1)
.
This term has the following explicit formula.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let

\vec{\gamma}=\gamma_{1}\times\gamma_{2}\times\gamma_{3}\times\gamma_{4}
and

\vec{\Gamma}=\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}\times\Gamma_{3}\times\Gamma_{4}
,
where
\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{4}
are small circles around
-1
, nested from inside to outside,
\Gamma_{1},\cdots,\Gamma_{4}
are small circles around
0
, also nested from inside to outside, with all circles mutually disjoint.
We have

\begin{split}\mathsf{Q}^{(1,2,1)}_{3}(\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T})=&\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{8}}\int_{\vec{\gamma}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{3122}\int_{\vec{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{1223}\,\Pi_{(1,2,1)}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}){\mathsf{F}}^{(1,2,1)}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})\\
&+\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{8}}\int_{\vec{\gamma}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}^{1223}\int_{\vec{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{3122}\,\Pi_{(1,2,1)}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}){\mathsf{F}}^{(1,2,1)}_{\mathbf{M},\mathbf{N},\mathbf{T}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})\end{split}

(3.16)

where
\boldsymbol{\xi}=(\xi^{1},\xi^{2}_{1},\xi^{2}_{2},\xi^{3})
,
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{3122}=(\xi^{3},\xi^{1},\xi^{2}_{1},\xi^{2}_{2})
,
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{1223}=(\xi^{1},\xi^{2}_{1},\xi^{2}_{2},\xi^{3})
, and similarly for
\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{3122},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{1223}
.

Proof.

When
m=3
and
\mathbf{n}=(1,2,1)
, we need to compute

\begin{split}\oint_{>1}\oint_{>1}&\left[\int_{C_{3,\textnormal{left}}^{\textnormal{in}}}\mathrm{d}\xi^{3}+z_{2}\int_{C_{3,\textnormal{left}}^{\textnormal{out}}}\mathrm{d}\xi^{3}\right]\left[\int_{C_{3,\textnormal{right}}^{\textnormal{in}}}\mathrm{d}\eta^{3}+z_{2}\int_{C_{3,\textnormal{right}}^{\textnormal{out}}}\mathrm{d}\eta^{3}\right]\\
&\prod_{i=1}^{2}\left[\int_{C_{2,\textnormal{left}}^{\textnormal{in}}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}^{2}+z_{1}\int_{C_{2,\textnormal{left}}^{\textnormal{out}}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}^{2}\right]\left[\int_{C_{2,\textnormal{right}}^{\textnormal{in}}}\mathrm{d}\eta_{i}^{2}+z_{1}\int_{C_{2,\textnormal{right}}^{\textnormal{out}}}\mathrm{d}\eta_{i}^{2}\right]\frac{\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathrm{d}z_{2}}{(z_{1}+1)^{2}z_{1}^{3}z_{2}^{2}}.\end{split}

Evaluating the
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
-integrals, we obtain the result.
∎

4
Miscellaneous lemmas

We record the following two lemmas, which will be used in several places throughout this paper.

Lemma 4.1

(
[
13
]
)
.

Let
m\geq 2
.
Let
\Gamma_{1},\cdots,\Gamma_{m}
be disjoint contours, listed from left to right, each parallel to the
y
-axis with upwards orientation.
Let
\vec{\Gamma}\equiv\Gamma_{1}\times\cdots\times\Gamma_{m}
.
For every
0=a_{0}<a_{1}<\cdots<a_{m}=A
and
b_{1},\cdots,b_{m-1}\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
b_{0}=b_{m}=0
,

\frac{\sqrt{2\pi A}}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{m}}\int_{\vec{\Gamma}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{m}e^{\frac{1}{2}(a_{i}-a_{i-1})u_{i}^{2}+(b_{i}-b_{i-1})u_{i}}}{\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}(u_{i+1}-u_{i})}\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{u}}=\mathbb{P}\left({\sqrt{A}\,\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}\left(\frac{{a_{i}}}{A}\right)>b_{i},\,\,i=1,\dots,m-1}\right)

where
\mathrm{d}{\mathbf{u}}=\mathrm{d}u_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}u_{m}
with each
u_{i}\in\Gamma_{i}
, and
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}
is a standard Brownian bridge.

Proof.

The equality can be verified by expressing the right-hand side in terms of the usual density function for a Brownian bridge, and then taking derivatives with respect to
b_{1},\cdots,b_{m-1}
. The details can be found in Lemma 3.4 of
[
13
]
.
∎

When proving the main theorems, we first establish them for parameters lying outside certain hypersurfaces.
We then extend the results to the full set of parameters using the next lemma.
The proof essentially follows that of Lemma 3.6 in
[
13
]
, although we present the result here in a slightly different form.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
I
be an open interval in
{\mathbb{R}}
and let
y_{0}\in I
.
For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
A_{n}
be an event, and let
\{Y_{n}(y)\}_{y\in I}
be a stochastic process.
Let
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
Suppose that the following two conditions hold:

1.

There is a continuous function
f
on
I
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\{Y_{n}(y)\leq r\}\cap A_{n})=f(y)\qquad\text{for every}\quad y\in I\setminus\{y_{0}\}.

(4.1)

2.

There is a continuous function
g
on
I\times I
satisfying
g(y,y)=0
for
y\in I
, such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(Y_{n}(y)\leq r,\,Y_{n}(y^{\prime})>r)=g(y,y^{\prime})\qquad\text{for every}\quad y,y^{\prime}\in I\quad\text{with}\quad y\neq y^{\prime}.

Then, (
4.1
) also holds for
y=y_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
y\in I\setminus\{y_{0}\}
.
Noting that

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(\{Y_{n}(y_{0})\leq r\}\cap A_{n})\leq\mathbb{P}(\{Y_{n}(y)\leq r\}\cap A_{n})+\mathbb{P}(Y_{n}(y_{0})\leq r,Y_{n}(y)>r),\end{split}

we find that

\begin{split}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\{Y_{n}(y_{0})\leq r\}\cap A_{n})\leq f(y)+g(y_{0},y).\end{split}

Similarly, since

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(\{Y_{n}(y_{0})\leq r\}\cap A_{n})\geq\mathbb{P}(\{Y_{n}(y)\leq r\}\cap A_{n})-\mathbb{P}(Y_{n}(y_{0})>r,Y_{n}(y)\leq r),\end{split}

we find that

\begin{split}\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\{Y_{n}(y_{0})\leq r\}\cap A_{n})\geq f(y)-g(y,y_{0}).\end{split}

Taking the limit as
y\to y_{0}
and using the continuity of
f
and
g
and the fact that
g(y_{0},y_{0})=0
, we conclude that
\mathbb{P}(\{Y_{n}(y_{0})\leq r\}\cap A_{n})
converges to
f(y_{0})
as
n\to\infty
.
∎

5
Asymptotic analysis of a function

When we evaluate the limits of the formulas in Proposition
3.1
, we require the asymptotic properties of the functions
f_{M,N,T}(z)=e^{N\log z-M\log(z+1)+Tz}
, defined in (
3.6
), as the parameters
M,N,T
tend to infinity.
In this section, we summarize the relevant asymptotic results for this function.

5.1
Asymptotic properties

Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
and
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
For every
L>0
, let
\delta^{1}_{L}
and
\delta^{2}_{L}
be real numbers such that

\alpha_{1}L+\beta_{1}L^{1/2}+\delta^{1}_{L}\in{\mathbb{N}},\qquad\alpha_{2}L+\beta_{2}L^{1/2}+\delta^{2}_{L}\in{\mathbb{N}},

and assume that
\delta^{1}_{L},\delta^{2}_{L}
are uniformly bounded for all
L>0
.
Define the functions

\begin{split}&\mathrm{G}(z)=-\alpha_{1}\log(z+1)+\alpha_{2}\log z+\alpha_{3}z,\\
&\mathrm{H}(z)=-\beta_{1}\log(z+1)+\beta_{2}\log z+\beta_{3}z,\\
&\mathrm{E}_{L}(z)=-\delta^{1}_{L}\log(z+1)+\delta^{2}_{L}\log z,\end{split}

(5.1)

and

{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z):=e^{L\mathrm{G}(z)+L^{1/2}\mathrm{H}(z)+\mathrm{E}_{L}(z)}.

(5.2)

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
z_{c}
be a critical point of
\mathrm{G}(z)
.
Then, for every
\epsilon\in(0,1/2)
, there exists a constant
L_{0}>0
such that for all
L>L_{0}
and
|w|\leq L^{\epsilon/3}
,

{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z_{c}+wL^{-1/2})={\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z_{c})e^{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})w^{2}+\mathrm{H}^{\prime}(z_{c})w}\big(1+O(L^{-1/2+\epsilon})\big).

(5.3)

Proof.

This follows from Taylor’s theorem expanded at
z=z_{c}
:

{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z)=f_{L}(z_{c})e^{L\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c})(z-z_{c})^{2}+O(|z-z_{c}|^{3})\right]+L^{1/2}\left[\mathrm{H}^{\prime}(z_{c})(z-z_{c})+O(|z-z_{c}|^{2})\right]+O(|z-z_{c}|)}.

∎

Lemma 5.2
.

The critical points of
\mathrm{G}(z)
are

z_{c}^{\pm}=\frac{-\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\pm\sqrt{\mathrm{Q}}}{2\alpha_{3}}\qquad\text{where}\quad\mathrm{Q}=\alpha_{3}^{2}-2(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\alpha_{3}+(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})^{2},

(5.4)

with the following cases:

1.

If
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
and
\alpha_{3}>(\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}+\sqrt{\alpha_{2}})^{2}
, then
\mathrm{Q}>0
,

\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c}^{+})<0
,
\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c}^{-})>0
, and
-1<z_{c}^{-}<z_{c}^{+}<0
.

2.

If
\alpha_{1}<0
and
\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}>0
, then
\mathrm{Q}>0
,
\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c}^{+})<0
,
\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c}^{-})<0
, and
z_{c}^{-}<-1<z_{c}^{+}<0
.

3.

If
\alpha_{2}<0
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}>0
then
\mathrm{Q}>0
,
\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c}^{+})>0
,
\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c}^{-})>0
, and
-1<z_{c}^{-}<0<z_{c}^{+}
.

Furthermore,

\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c}^{\pm})=\mp\frac{\sqrt{\mathrm{Q}}}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\left[(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\alpha_{3}-(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})^{2}\pm(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})\sqrt{\mathrm{Q}}\right].

(5.5)

Proof.

Since

\mathrm{G}^{\prime}(z)=-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{z+1}+\frac{\alpha_{2}}{z}+\alpha_{3}=\frac{\alpha_{3}z^{2}+(\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})z+\alpha_{2}}{(z+1)z},

we obtain (
5.4
).
It is also direct to verify (
5.5
).
Set
\mathrm{A}_{\pm}=\mp\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c}^{\pm})
.
Note that
\mathrm{A}_{+}\mathrm{A}_{-}=\frac{\alpha_{3}^{2}\mathrm{Q}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}
.

1.

Suppose
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
and
\alpha_{3}>(\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}+\sqrt{\alpha_{2}})^{2}
.
Since
\mathrm{Q}=(\alpha_{3}-(\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}+\sqrt{\alpha_{2}})^{2})(\alpha_{3}-(\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}-\sqrt{\alpha_{2}})^{2})
,
the condition
\alpha_{3}>(\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}+\sqrt{\alpha_{2}})^{2}
implies
\mathrm{Q}>0
.
The same condition also implies that

\frac{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{\sqrt{\mathrm{Q}}}(\mathrm{A}_{+}+\mathrm{A}_{-})=(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\alpha_{3}-(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})^{2}>2\sqrt{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}(\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}+\sqrt{\alpha_{2}})^{2}>0.

Since
\mathrm{A}_{+}\mathrm{A}_{-}=\frac{\alpha_{3}^{2}\mathrm{Q}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}>0
, we find that
\mathrm{A}_{\pm}>0
.
The inequalities
-1<z_{c}^{-}<z_{c}^{+}<0
follow from the inequalities
\sqrt{\mathrm{Q}}<\alpha_{3}\pm(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})
, which can be checked by squaring both sides.

2.

Suppose
\alpha_{1}<0
and
\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}>0
.
Since
\mathrm{Q}=(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})^{2}-4\alpha_{1}\alpha_{3}
, we find that
\mathrm{Q}>0
.
In this case,
\mathrm{A}_{+}\mathrm{A}_{-}=\frac{\alpha_{3}^{2}\mathrm{Q}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}<0
and
\mathrm{A}_{+}>\mathrm{A}_{-}
. Thus,
A_{+}>0
and
A_{-}<0
.
The property
z_{c}^{-}<-1<z_{c}^{+}<0
follows from the inequalities
|\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}|<\sqrt{\mathrm{Q}}<\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
.

3.

Suppose
\alpha_{2}<0
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}>0
.
From
\mathrm{Q}=(\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})^{2}-4\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}
, we see that
\mathrm{Q}>0
.
Since
\mathrm{A}_{+}\mathrm{A}_{-}=\frac{\alpha_{3}^{2}\mathrm{Q}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}<0
and
\mathrm{A}_{+}<\mathrm{A}_{-}
, it follows that
\mathrm{A}_{+}<0
and
\mathrm{A}_{-}>0
.
The property
-1<z_{c}^{-}<0<z_{c}^{+}
follows from noting that
|\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}|<\sqrt{\mathrm{Q}}<\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}
.

∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
z_{c}^{\pm}
be the critical points of
\mathrm{G}(z)
as given in (
5.4
).
Let
b\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and for each
L>0
, define the circles

\Sigma^{b,L}_{-}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\,:\,|z+1|=|z_{c}^{-}+1|+bL^{-\frac{1}{2}}\},\qquad\Sigma^{b,L}_{+}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\,:\,|z|=|z_{c}^{+}|+bL^{-\frac{1}{2}}\}.

(5.6)

•

If
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
and
\alpha_{3}>(\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}+\sqrt{\alpha_{2}})^{2}
, then both statements
1
and
2
below hold.

•

If
\alpha_{1}<0
and
\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}>0
, then statement
2
holds.

•

If
\alpha_{2}<0
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}>0
, then statement
1
holds.

1.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that for every
0<\epsilon<1/2
,

\left|\frac{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z)}{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z_{c}^{-})}\right|=O(e^{-cL^{2\epsilon/3}})\qquad\text{for $z\in\Sigma^{b,L}_{-}\cap\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z-z_{c}^{-}|\geq L^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{3}}\}$}

(5.7)

as
L\to\infty
. Furthermore, there exist
L_{0}>0
and
C>0
such that for all
L>L_{0}
,

\left|\frac{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z)}{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z_{c}^{-})}\right|\leq C\quad\text{for $z\in\Sigma^{b,L}_{-}$}.

(5.8)

2.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that for every
0<\epsilon<1/2
,

\left|\frac{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z_{c}^{+})}{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z)}\right|=O(e^{-cL^{2\epsilon/3}})\qquad\text{for $z\in\Sigma^{b,L}_{+}\cap\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z-z_{c}^{+}|\geq L^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{3}}\}$}

(5.9)

as
L\to\infty
.
Furthermore, there exist
L_{0}>0
and
C>0
such that for all
L>L_{0}
,

\left|\frac{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z_{c}^{+})}{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z)}\right|\leq C\quad\text{for $z\in\Sigma^{b,L}_{+}$}.

(5.10)

5.2
Proof of Lemma
5.3

We use the following result.

Lemma 5.4
.

1.

If
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
and
\alpha_{3}>(\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}+\sqrt{\alpha_{2}})^{2}
, then

|z_{c}^{-}+1|<s_{-}:=1-\sqrt{\alpha_{2}/\alpha_{3}},\qquad|z_{c}^{+}|<s_{+}:=1-\sqrt{\alpha_{1}/\alpha_{3}},

(5.11)

and, for every
s\in(0,\mathrm{s}_{-})
,

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\re\mathrm{G}(-1+se^{\textnormal{\text{i}}\theta})<0\quad\text{for $\theta\in(0,\pi)$;}\qquad\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\re\mathrm{G}(-1+se^{\textnormal{\text{i}}\theta})>0\quad\text{for $\theta\in(-\pi,0)$,}\end{split}

(5.12)

and, for every
s\in(0,\mathrm{s}_{+})
,

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\re G_{1}(se^{\textnormal{\text{i}}\theta})<0\quad\text{for $\theta\in(0,\pi)$;}\qquad\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\re G_{1}(se^{\textnormal{\text{i}}\theta})>0\quad\text{for $\theta\in(-\pi,0)$.}\end{split}

(5.13)

2.

If
\alpha_{1}<0
and
\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}>0
, then (
5.13
) holds with
\mathrm{s}_{+}=1
.

3.

If
\alpha_{2}<0
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}>0
, then (
5.12
) holds with
\mathrm{s}_{-}=1
.

Proof.

(a) Suppose
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
and
\alpha_{3}>(\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}+\sqrt{\alpha_{2}})^{2}
.
From the formula (
5.4
) for
z_{c}^{\pm}
,
the properties
\mathrm{s}_{-}>|z_{c}^{-}+1|=z_{c}^{-}+1
and
\mathrm{s}_{+}>|z_{c}^{+}|=-z_{c}^{+}
hold since

\begin{split}(\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\sqrt{\mathrm{Q}})^{2}-4\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}&=2\mathrm{Q}+2(\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\sqrt{\mathrm{Q}}>0,\\
(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}+\sqrt{\mathrm{Q}})^{2}-4\alpha_{1}\alpha_{3}&=2\mathrm{Q}+2(\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})\sqrt{\mathrm{Q}}>0.\end{split}

From the formula of
\mathrm{G}
, we have

\begin{split}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\re\mathrm{G}(-1+se^{\textnormal{\text{i}}\theta})=s\sin\theta\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{1+s^{2}-2s\cos\theta}-\alpha_{3}\right),\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\re\mathrm{G}(se^{\textnormal{\text{i}}\theta})=s\sin\theta\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{1+s^{2}+2s\cos\theta}-\alpha_{3}\right).\end{split}

Note that
0<s_{\pm}<1
. If
s\in(0,\mathrm{s}_{-})
, then

\frac{\alpha_{2}}{1+s^{2}-2s\cos\theta}-\alpha_{3}\leq\frac{\alpha_{2}}{(1-s)^{2}}-\alpha_{3}<0

for every
\theta
. Similarly, if
s\in(0,\mathrm{s}_{+})
, then

\frac{\alpha_{1}}{1+s^{2}+2s\cos\theta}-\alpha_{3}\leq\frac{\alpha_{1}}{(1-s)^{2}}-\alpha_{3}<0

for every
\theta
. Hence, (
5.12
) and (
5.13
) hold.

(b) If
\alpha_{1}<0
and
\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}>0
, then

\frac{\alpha_{1}}{1+s^{2}+2s\cos\theta}-\alpha_{3}\leq\frac{\alpha_{1}}{(1+s)^{2}}-\alpha_{3}<0

for every
s\in(0,1)
and all
\theta
. Thus, (
5.13
) holds with
s_{+}=1
.

(c) If
\alpha_{2}<0
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}>0
, then

\frac{\alpha_{2}}{1+s^{2}-2s\cos\theta}-\alpha_{3}\leq\frac{\alpha_{2}}{(1+s)^{2}}-\alpha_{3}<0

for every
s\in(0,1)
and all
\theta
. Thus, (
5.12
) holds with
s_{-}=1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

\bullet
Suppose
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
and
\alpha_{3}>(\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}+\sqrt{\alpha_{2}})^{2}
.
Let
\mathrm{A}_{\pm}=\mp\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z_{c}^{\pm})
.
By Lemma
5.2
(a), we have
A_{\pm}>0
.
By Taylor’s theorem at
z=z_{c}^{\pm}
, there exists
\delta>0
such that

\mathrm{G}(z)-\mathrm{G}(z_{c}^{\pm})=\mp\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\pm}}{2}(z-z_{c}^{\pm})^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{1,\pm}(z)

where the function
\mathcal{E}_{1,\pm}(z)
satisfies

|\mathcal{E}_{1,\pm}(z)|\leq\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\pm}}{8}|z-z^{\pm}_{c}|^{2}
for
|z-z_{c}^{\pm}|\leq\delta
.
Note that
\re(w^{2})\leq-\frac{1}{2}|w|^{2}
if
\arg w\in[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}]\cup[\frac{4\pi}{3},\frac{5\pi}{3}]
, since
\cos(2\theta)\leq\cos(\frac{2\pi}{3})\leq-\frac{1}{2}
for such
\arg w=\theta
.
Thus,

\begin{split}&\mp\re(\mathrm{G}(z)-\mathrm{G}(z_{c}^{\pm}))=\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\pm}}{2}\re[(z-z_{c}^{\pm})^{2}]+\re(\mp\mathcal{E}_{1,\pm}(z))\leq-\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\pm}}{8}|z-z_{c}^{\pm}|^{2}\quad\text{for $|z-z_{c}^{\pm}|\leq\delta$}\end{split}

(5.14)

whenever

\arg(z-z_{c}^{\pm})\in\left[\pi/3,2\pi/3\right]\cup\left[4\pi/3,5\pi/3\right].

Moreover, possibly after shrinking
\delta>0
, there exists
C>0

such that

|\mathrm{H}(z)-\mathrm{H}(z^{\pm}_{c})|\leq C|z-z_{c}^{\pm}|\quad\text{for $|z-z_{c}^{\pm}|\leq\delta$,}

(5.15)

and

|\mathrm{E}_{L}(z)-\mathrm{E}_{L}(z_{c}^{\pm})|\leq C\quad\text{for $|z-z_{c}^{\pm}|\leq\delta$ and for every $L>0$.}

(5.16)

Fix
\epsilon\in(0,1/2)
, and divide the circle
\Sigma^{b,L}_{\pm}
into two parts:

\begin{split}&\Sigma^{b,L,1}_{\pm}:=\Sigma^{b,L}_{\pm}\cap\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:0\leq|z-z_{c}^{\pm}|\leq\delta\},\qquad\Sigma^{b,L,2}_{\pm}:=\Sigma^{b,L}_{\pm}\cap\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z-z_{c}^{\pm}|\geq\delta\}.\end{split}

(5.17)

Since the circles
\Sigma^{b,L}_{\pm}
are close to vertical lines near the points
z_{c}^{\pm}
,
after adjusting
\delta>0
if necessary,
we have
\arg(z-z^{\pm}_{c})\in[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}]\cup[\frac{4\pi}{3},\frac{5\pi}{3}]
for
z\in\Sigma^{b,L,1}_{\pm}
and for all sufficiently large
L>0
.
Therefore, from (
5.14
), (
5.15
), and (
5.16
), there exist
L_{0}>0
such that

\mp\log\left|\frac{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z)}{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z^{\pm}_{c})}\right|\leq-\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\pm}}{8}|z-z^{\pm}_{c}|^{2}L+C|z-z^{\pm}_{c}|L^{1/2}+C\qquad\text{for $z\in\Sigma^{b,L,1}_{\pm}$}

(5.18)

and for every
L\geq L_{0}
.
Thus,
\mp\log\left|\frac{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z)}{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z^{\pm}_{c})}\right|
is uniformly bounded from the above on
\Sigma^{b,L,1}_{\pm}
for all
L\geq L_{0}
. We also note that there exists
L_{1}>0
such that

-\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\pm}}{8}|z-z^{\pm}_{c}|^{2}L+C|z-z^{\pm}_{c}|L^{1/2}\leq-\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\pm}}{16}|z-z^{\pm}_{c}|^{2}L\leq-\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\pm}}{16}L^{\frac{2\epsilon}{3}}

if
|z-z^{\pm}_{c}|\geq L^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{3}}
, for all
L\geq L_{1}
.

Now we consider the part
\Sigma^{b,L,2}_{\pm}
.
Let
z_{1}^{\pm}
denote the endpoint of the arc
\Sigma^{b,L,1}_{\pm}
in the upper half-plane.
Because
\alpha_{3}>(\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}+\sqrt{\alpha_{2}})^{2}
,
the inequalities (
5.11
) holds.
Thus, setting
s=|1+z_{c}^{-}|+bL^{-1/2}
or
s=|z_{c}^{+}|+bL^{-1/2}
,
the properties (
5.12
) and (
5.13
) hold for all
z\in\Gamma_{\pm}^{b,L,2}
by Lemma
5.4
, for all large enough
L
.
Hence, noting that
\re\mathrm{G}(z)=\re\mathrm{G}(\bar{z})
, we find that there exists
L_{2}>0
such that

\mp\re\left(\mathrm{G}(z)-\mathrm{G}(z_{c}^{\pm})\right)\leq\mp\re\left(\mathrm{G}(z_{1}^{\pm})-\mathrm{G}(z_{c}^{\pm})\right)\text{ for every }z\in\Sigma^{b,L,2}_{\pm}

(5.19)

for all
L\geq L_{2}
.
From (
5.14
), we see that

\mp\re\left(\mathrm{G}(z^{\pm}_{1})-\mathrm{G}(z_{c}^{\pm})\right)\leq-\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\pm}}{8}|z^{\pm}_{1}-z^{\pm}_{c}|^{2}=-\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\pm}}{8}\delta^{2}
.
Since
\Sigma^{b,L,2}_{\pm}
lies in a compact subset of
{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{-1,0\}
for all sufficiently large
L
, we find that
there exist
L_{3}>0
and
K>0
such that

\mp\log\left|\frac{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(z)}{{\mathsf{f}}_{L}(\mathrm{z}^{\pm}_{c})}\right|\leq-\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\pm}}{8}\delta^{2}L+KL^{1/2}

(5.20)

for every
z
in the arc
\Sigma^{b,L,2}_{\pm}
, whenever
L\geq L_{3}
.

The estimates (
5.7
), (
5.8
), (
5.9
), and (
5.10
) follow from the above computations.

\bullet
Suppose
\alpha_{1}<0
and
\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}>0
. By Lemma
5.2
(b), we have
A_{+}>0
.
The proof of (b) for the
(+)
-case applies here as well, and thus the result follows.

\bullet
Suppose
\alpha_{2}<0
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}>0
.
by Lemma
5.2
(c), we have
A_{-}>0
.
The proof of (a) for the
(-)
-case applies here as well, and thus the result follows.
∎

6
Proof of Theorem
1.4

To prove the theorem, we show that for every
m\geq 2
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{m-1}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{L}(t_{i}aL+x_{i}\frac{a(\ell-a+b)}{\ell\sqrt{D}}\mathsf{\sigma}L^{1/2},t_{i}bL-x_{i}\frac{b(\ell+a-b)}{\ell\sqrt{D}}\mathsf{\sigma}L^{1/2})-t_{i}\ell L}{\mathsf{\sigma}L^{1/2}}>h_{i}\right\}\,\bigg|\,\mathcal{L}(aL,bL)=\ell L\right)

(6.1)

converges, as
L\to\infty
, to

\mathsf{P}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{h}):=\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{m-1}\left\{\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{1}(t_{i})-\left|\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{2}(t_{i})-x_{i}\right|\}>h_{i}\right\}\right)

for every

\mathbf{t}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{m-1})\in(0,1)^{m-1},\quad\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m-1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m-1},\quad\mathbf{h}=(h_{1},\cdots,h_{m-1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m-1}.

(6.2)

Here we use
L
as the large parameter, whereas in the theorem we used
N
.
Using the identity
\min(a,b)=\frac{a+b}{2}-\frac{|a-b|}{2}
,

\mathsf{P}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{h})=\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{m-1}\left\{\min\{\sqrt{2}\mathsf{c}_{+}\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{+}(t_{i})-x_{i},\sqrt{2}\mathsf{c}_{-}\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{-}(t_{i})+x_{i}\}>h_{i}\right\}\right)

(6.3)

where
\mathsf{c}_{\pm}
are defined in (
1.14
) and
\mathbb{B}^{\mathrm{br}}_{\pm}
are independent Brownian bridges.

Since the limit (
6.3
) is a continuous function of
t_{1},\cdots,t_{m-1}
, successive applications of Lemma
4.2
imply that, if the result holds for the case when
t_{i}\neq t_{j}
for every
i\neq j
, then it also holds for all
t_{1},\cdots,t_{m-1}\in(0,1)
.
Thus, it suffices to assume that all
t_{i}
are distinct. By re-labelling the indices if necessary, we may further assume that
t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m-1}
.
We now prove that (
6.1
) converges to (
6.3
) under this assumption.

Fix an integer
m\geq 2
and fix the numbers (
6.2
), assuming now that

0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{m-1}<1.

We use
L
as the large parameter instead of
N
. For real numbers
L>0
, define

\begin{split}&\mathbf{M}_{L}=(M_{L,1},\cdots,M_{L,{m}})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m},\qquad\mathbf{N}_{L}=(N_{L,1},\cdots,N_{L,{m}})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m},\qquad\mathbf{T}_{L}=(T_{L,1},\cdots,T_{L,{m}})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{m}\end{split}

where
9
9

9

Recall that
\lceil s\rceil
denotes the least integer greater than or equal to
s
.
for
i=1,\cdots,m-1
,

M_{L,i}=\big\lceil t_{i}aL+x_{i}\frac{a(\ell-a+b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}L^{1/2}\rceil,\quad N_{L,i}=\big\lceil t_{i}bL-x_{i}\frac{b(\ell+a-b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}L^{1/2}\big\rceil,\quad T_{L,i}=t_{i}\ell L+h_{i}\mathsf{\sigma}L^{1/2},

(6.4)

and

M_{L,m}=\lceil aL\rceil,\qquad N_{L,m}=\lceil bL\rceil,\qquad T_{L,m}=\ell L.

We also set
M_{L,0}=N_{L,0}=T_{L,0}=0
.

Recalling
(
1.1
)
, Proposition
3.1
implies that Theorem
1.4
is proved if we show that

\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{\mathsf{Q}_{m}(\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L})}{\mathsf{Q}_{1}(M_{L,m},N_{L,m},T_{L,m})}=\mathsf{P}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{h}).

(6.5)

Recall that

\mathsf{Q}_{m}(\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L})=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}}\frac{1}{(\mathbf{n}!)^{2}}\mathsf{Q}_{m}^{(\mathbf{n})}(\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L})

(6.6)

where
\mathsf{Q}_{m}^{(\mathbf{n})}
is given by the formula (
3.11
).
The following lemma shows that the term with
\mathbf{n}=(1,\cdots,1)
is responsible for the limit.
For
L>0
, define the constant

\mathsf{Z}_{L}:=\left(\frac{\ell+a-b+\sqrt{D}}{\ell+a-b-\sqrt{D}}\right)^{\lceil aL\rceil}\left(\frac{\ell-a+b+\sqrt{D}}{\ell-a+b-\sqrt{D}}\right)^{\lceil bL\rceil}e^{-\sqrt{D}L},

(6.7)

where
D
is defined in (
1.7
).

Lemma 6.1
.

Set
\mathbf{1}=(1,\cdots,1)
. We have

\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{2\pi LD}{\sqrt{ab}\mathsf{Z}_{L}}\mathsf{Q}_{m}^{(\mathbf{1})}(\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L})=\mathsf{P}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{h}).

The next result shows that the remaining terms in the sum are negligible by comparison.

Lemma 6.2
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that

\frac{1}{\mathsf{Z}_{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{m}\setminus\{\mathbf{1}\}}\frac{1}{(\mathbf{n}!)^{2}}\left|\mathsf{Q}_{m}^{(\mathbf{n})}(\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L})\right|\leq e^{-cL}

for all sufficiently large
L>0
.

The same analysis applies to the case when
m=1
. Note that in this case,
\mathsf{P}(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{h})=1
.

Lemma 6.3
.

We have

\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{2\pi LD}{\sqrt{ab}\mathsf{Z}_{L}}\mathsf{Q}_{1}(aL,bL,\ell L)=1.

The above three lemmas complete the proof of Theorem
1.4
.
We prove Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2
in Subsections
6.2
and
6.3
, respectively, following a preliminary discussion of some functions in Subsection
6.1
.
Lemma
6.3
is the special case
m=1
of these two lemmas, and we omit its proof.

6.1
Formula of
{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}

The quantity
\mathsf{Q}_{m}^{(\mathbf{n})}
in (
6.6
) is expressed in terms of
\mathsf{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L}}(\mathbf{z})
from (
3.8
), which involves the functions

{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(z):=\frac{f_{M_{L,i},N_{L,i},T_{L,i}}(z)}{f_{M_{L,i-1},N_{L,i-1},T_{L,i-1}}(z)},\qquad f_{M,N,T}(z)=\frac{z^{N}e^{Tz}}{(z+1)^{M}}=e^{-M\log(z+1)+N\log z+Tz}.

From (
6.4
), we have

M_{L,i}=t_{i}aL+x_{i}\frac{a(\ell-a+b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}L^{1/2}+\epsilon_{L,i}\qquad N_{L,i}=t_{i}bL-x_{i}\frac{b(\ell+a-b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}L^{1/2}+\epsilon^{\prime}_{L,i}

(6.8)

for real numbers
\epsilon_{L,i},\epsilon_{L,i}^{\prime}\in[0,1)
.
Thus,

{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(z)=e^{(t_{i}-t_{i-1})\mathrm{G}(z)L+\mathrm{H}_{i}(z)L^{1/2}+\mathrm{E}_{L,i}(z)}

(6.9)

where

\begin{split}&\mathrm{G}(z)=-a\log(z+1)+b\log z+\ell z,\\
&\mathrm{H}_{i}(z)=-(x_{i}-x_{i-1})\frac{a(\ell-a+b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}\log(z+1)-(x_{i}-x_{i-1})\frac{b(\ell+a-b)\mathsf{\sigma}}{\ell\sqrt{D}}\log z+(h_{i}-h_{i-1})\mathsf{\sigma}z,\\
&\mathrm{E}_{L,i}(z)=-\delta^{1}_{L,i}\log(z+1)+\delta^{2}_{L,i}\log z,\end{split}

(6.10)

with real numbers

\delta^{1}_{L,i},\,\delta^{2}_{L,i}\in(-1,1).

(6.11)

Here, we set
x_{0}=t_{0}=h_{0}=x_{m}=h_{m}=0
and
t_{m}=1
.
Note that
{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(z)
are analytic except possibly at
z=0
and
z=-1
.

We list a few properties:

•

From Lemma
5.2
, the critical points of
\mathrm{G}
are

z^{\pm}=\frac{-\ell+a-b\pm\sqrt{D}}{2\ell},\qquad D=\ell^{2}-(a+b)\ell+(a-b)^{2},

(6.12)

and they satisfy the inequalities
-1<z^{-}<z^{+}<0
.

•

It is straightforward to check (see (
5.5
)) that

\mathrm{G}^{\prime\prime}(z^{\pm})=\mp\frac{\sqrt{D}}{2ab}\left[(a+b)\ell-(a-b)^{2}\pm(a-b)\sqrt{D}\right]=\mp 2\mathsf{c}_{\pm}^{2}\mathsf{\sigma}^{2},

(6.13)

and that

\mathrm{H}_{i}^{\prime}(z^{\pm})=\mathsf{\sigma}\left(\pm(x_{i}-x_{i-1})+h_{i}-h_{i-1}\right).

(6.14)

•

Since
\prod_{i=1}^{m}{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(z)=f_{M_{L,m},N_{L,m},T_{L,m}}(z)
, we see that

\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(z^{-})}{{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(z^{+})}=\frac{f_{M_{L,m},N_{L,m},T_{L,m}}(z^{-})}{f_{M_{L,m},N_{L,m},T_{L,m}}(z^{+})}=\mathsf{Z}_{L},

(6.15)

where
\mathsf{Z}_{L}
is defined in (
6.7
).

•

It is direct to see that

\mathrm{G}(z^{+})-\mathrm{G}(z^{-})=\sqrt{D}+a\log\bigg(\frac{\ell+a-b-\sqrt{D}}{\ell+a-b+\sqrt{D}}\bigg)+b\log\bigg(\frac{\ell-a+b-\sqrt{D}}{\ell-a+b+\sqrt{D}}\bigg)=\mathsf{J}(\ell),

(6.16)

using the notation from (
1.6
). In particular,
\mathsf{J}(\ell)>0
.
Since
t_{i}-t_{i-1}>0
for every
i
, there exists
L_{0}>0
such that

\frac{{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(z^{-})}{{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(z^{+})}\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}(t_{i}-t_{i-1})(\mathrm{G}(z^{+})-\mathrm{G}(z^{-}))L}\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\tau\mathsf{J}(\ell)L},\qquad\tau:=\min_{1\leq i\leq m}(t_{i}-t_{i-1})>0,

(6.17)

for every
L>L_{0}
and
i=1,\cdots,m
.

6.2
Proof of Lemma
6.1

By Lemma
3.3
,

\mathsf{Q}_{m}^{(\mathbf{1})}(\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L})=-\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2m}}\int_{\vec{\gamma}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}\int_{\vec{\Gamma}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\eta}\,\,\Pi_{\mathbf{1}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}){\mathsf{F}}^{(\mathbf{1})}_{\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}).

(6.18)

By Cauchy’s theorem, we can deform the contours, without changing the value of the integral, to

\vec{\gamma}=\Sigma_{-}^{1,L}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{-}^{m,L},\qquad\vec{\Gamma}=\Sigma_{+}^{1,L}\times\cdots\times\Sigma_{+}^{m,L}.

where
\Sigma^{i,L}_{\pm}
are the circles in Lemma
5.3
with
z^{\pm}_{c}=z^{\pm}
and
b=i
.
Note that all circles
\Sigma_{\pm}^{i,L}
are contained in the disk
\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z|\leq 2\}
for all sufficiently large
L>0
.
Fix
\epsilon\in(0,1/2)
and define
D_{L,-}^{\epsilon}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z-z^{-}|\leq L^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{3}}\}
and
D_{L,+}^{\epsilon}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z-z^{+}|\leq L^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{3}}\}
. Set

\vec{\gamma}^{\epsilon}=(\Sigma_{-}^{1,L}\cap D_{L,-}^{\epsilon})\times\cdots\times(\Sigma_{-}^{m,L}\cap D_{L,-}^{\epsilon}),\qquad\vec{\Gamma}^{\epsilon}=(\Sigma_{+}^{1,L}\cap D_{L,+}^{\epsilon})\times\cdots\times(\Sigma_{+}^{m,L}\cap D_{L,+}^{\epsilon}).

Since
a,b>0
and
\ell>(\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b})^{2}
, Lemma
5.3
(a) and (b) apply to
{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(z)
.
Thus, using (
6.15
),

\frac{|{\mathsf{F}}^{(\mathbf{1})}_{\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})|}{\mathsf{Z}_{L}}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left|\frac{{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(\xi^{i}){\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(z^{+})}{{\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(z^{-}){\mathsf{f}}_{L,i}(\eta^{i})}\right|=O(e^{-cL^{2\epsilon/3}})\quad\text{uniformly for $(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})\in(\vec{\gamma}\times\vec{\Gamma})\setminus(\vec{\gamma}^{\epsilon}\times\vec{\Gamma}^{\epsilon})$.}

On the other hand, note that

\dist(\Sigma^{-}_{L,i},\Sigma^{-}_{L,j})\geq L^{-\frac{1}{2}},\qquad\dist(\Sigma^{+}_{L,i},\Sigma^{+}_{L,j})\geq L^{-\frac{1}{2}}

for every
i\neq j
, and
\dist(\Sigma^{-}_{L,i},\Sigma^{+}_{L,j})
is bounded below by a constant for all
i,j
and sufficiently large
L
.
Since all circles are contained in the disk
\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z|\leq 2\}
when
L
is large enough,
we find from (
3.15
) that

\Pi_{\mathbf{1}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})=O(L^{m-1})\qquad\text{uniformly for $(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})\in\vec{\gamma}\times\vec{\Gamma}$}

as
L\to\infty
.
Thus,

\frac{1}{\mathsf{Z}_{L}L^{m-1}}\int_{(\vec{\gamma}\times\vec{\Gamma})\setminus(\vec{\gamma}^{\epsilon}\times\vec{\Gamma}^{\epsilon})}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\eta}\,\Pi_{\mathbf{1}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}){\mathsf{F}}^{(\mathbf{1})}_{\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})=O(e^{-cL^{2\epsilon/3}}).

(6.19)

We now evaluate the integral over
\vec{\gamma}^{\epsilon}\times\vec{\Gamma}^{\epsilon}
. Changing the variables as
\xi^{i}\mapsto u_{i}
and
\eta^{i}\mapsto v_{i}
given by

\xi^{i}=z^{-}+\frac{u_{i}}{\mathsf{\sigma}L^{1/2}},\qquad\eta^{i}=z^{+}-\frac{v_{i}}{\mathsf{\sigma}L^{1/2}},

(6.20)

we have

\int_{\vec{\gamma}^{\epsilon}\times\vec{\Gamma}^{\epsilon}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\xi}\,\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\eta}\,\Pi_{\mathbf{1}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta}){\mathsf{F}}^{(\mathbf{1})}_{\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L}}(\boldsymbol{\xi},\boldsymbol{\eta})=\frac{(-1)^{m}}{(\mathsf{\sigma}^{2}L)^{m}}\int_{\vec{\Sigma}^{-}_{L}\times\vec{\Sigma}^{+}_{L}}\hat{\Pi}_{\mathbf{1}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\hat{\mathsf{F}}_{L}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\,\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{u}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbf{v}

(6.21)

where
\hat{\Pi}_{\mathbf{1}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=\Pi_{\mathbf{1}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\mathbf{u}),\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{v}))

and
\hat{\mathsf{F}}_{L}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})={\mathsf{F}}^{(\mathbf{1})}_{\mathbf{M}_{L},\mathbf{N}_{L},\mathbf{T}_{L}}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(\mathbf{u}),\boldsymbol{\eta}(\mathbf{v}))
,
and the contours
\vec{\Sigma}^{-}_{L}
and
\vec{\Sigma}^{+}_{L}
are the images of the contours
\vec{\gamma}^{\epsilon}
and
\vec{\Gamma}^{\epsilon}
under the change of variables.
Noting that
z^{+}-z^{-}=\frac{\sqrt{D}}{\ell}
, we find from (
3.15
) that

\hat{\Pi}_{\mathbf{1}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})=\frac{\ell(\mathsf{\sigma}^{2}L)^{m-1}}{\sqrt{D}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{(u_{i}-u_{i+1})(v_{i+1}-v_{i})}\right]\left(1+O(L^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{3}})\right)\qquad\text{for $(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\in\vec{\Sigma}^{-}_{L}\times\vec{\Sigma}^{+}_{L}$.}

On the other hand, using (
6.13
) and (
6.14
), Lemma
5.1
gives

\frac{\hat{\mathsf{F}}_{L}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})}{\mathsf{Z}_{L}}={\mathsf{F}}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\left(1+O(L^{-1/2+\epsilon})\right)\qquad\text{for $(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\in\vec{\Sigma}^{-}_{L}\times\vec{\Sigma}^{+}_{L}$,}

where
